Crash-Stop Failures in Asynchronous Multiparty Session Types
Adam D. Barwell ORCID:0000-0003-1236-7160, Ping Hou ORCID:0000-0001-6899-9971, Nobuko Yoshida ORCID:0000-0002-3925-8557, Fangyi Zhou ORCID:0000-0002-8973-0821

Abstract
. Session types provide a typing discipline for message-passing systems. However, their theory often assumes an ideal world: one in which everything is reliable and without failures. Yet this is in stark contrast with distributed systems in the real world. To address this limitation, we introduce a new asynchronous multiparty session types (MPST) theory with crash-stop failures , where processes may crash arbitrarily and cease to interact after crashing. We augment asynchronous MPST and processes with crash handling branches, and integrate crash-stop failure semantics into types and processes. Our approach requires no user-level syntax extensions for global types, and features a formalisation of global semantics, which captures complex behaviours induced by crashed/crash handling processes. Our new theory covers the entire spectrum of unreliability, ranging from the ideal world of total reliability to entirely unreliable scenarios where any process may crash, using optional reliability assumptions . Under these assumptions, we demonstrate the sound and complete correspondence between global and local type semantics, which guarantee deadlock-freedom, protocol conformance, and liveness of well-typed processes by construction , even in the presence of crashes.

1.
Introduction

Background

As distributed programming grows increasingly prevalent,
significant research effort has been devoted to improve the reliability of
distributed systems.
A key aspect of this research focuses on studying

un
reliability (or, more specifically, failures).
Modelling unreliability and failures
enables a distributed system to be designed to be more tolerant of failures,
and thus more resilient.

In pursuit of methods to achieve fundamental
reliability –
safety

in distributed communication systems –

session types

[
ESOP98Session
]
provide a lightweight,
type system–based approach to message-passing concurrency.
In particular,

Multiparty Session Types
(MPST)
[
HYC08
]
facilitate the
specification and verification of communication between message-passing
processes in concurrent and distributed systems.
The typing discipline
prevents common communication-based errors,
e.g. deadlocks and communication mismatches
[
HYC16
,
POPL19LessIsMore
]
.

Nevertheless, the challenge to account for unreliability and failures persists
for session types:
most session type systems assume that both participants and message transmissions
are
reliable
, i.e. without failures.
In a real-world setting, however, participants may crash, communication channels may fail, and messages may be lost.
The lack of failure modelling in session type theories prevents
their application to large-scale distributed systems.

Recent works
[
OOPSLA21FaultTolerantMPST
,
FORTE22FaultTolerant
,
ECOOP22AffineMPST
,
ESOP23MAGPi
,
CONCUR22MPSTCrash
]

address the gap in failure modelling within session
types with various techniques.
Viering
et al.

[
OOPSLA21FaultTolerantMPST
]
introduce
failure
suspicion
,
where a participant may suspect their communication partner has failed,
and act accordingly.
Peters
et al.

[
FORTE22FaultTolerant
]
introduce
reliability
annotations
at type level, and fall back to a given
default
value in
case of failures.
Lagaillardie
et al.

[
ECOOP22AffineMPST
]
propose a framework of
affine

multiparty session types, where a session can terminate prematurely, e.g. in case of failures.
Barwell
et al.

[
CONCUR22MPSTCrash
]
incorporate
crash-stop failures

in session types, where a generalised type system validates safety and liveness
properties. Le Brun and Dardha
[
ESOP23MAGPi
]

adopt a similar approach but extend it to include additional failure types, e.g. message losses, reordering, and delays.

This Paper

Unlike the aforementioned approaches, we advance failure modelling in session types
by introducing a new asynchronous multiparty session type theory
that incorporates
crash-stop
failures
[
DBLP:books/daglib/0025983
, §2.2]
, where
a process may crash and cease to interact with others.
This model is standard in distributed systems, and is used in
related work on session types with error-handling
capabilities
[
ESOP18CrashHandling
,
OOPSLA21FaultTolerantMPST
]
.
However, unlike previous work,
we allow
any
process to crash arbitrarily,
and
support optional assumptions on non-crashing processes.
Our extended asynchronous MPST theory
effectively models failures with crash-stop
semantics,
and demonstrates that the usual session type guarantees remain
valid, i.e. communication safety, deadlock-freedom, and liveness.

In our new theory, we add
crashing
and
crash handling

semantics to processes and types.
With
minimal
changes to the standard surface syntax compared with the
original MPST theory,
we
model a variety of subtle, complex behaviours arising from
unreliable communicating
processes.
An active process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
may crash arbitrarily, and a process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}

interacting with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
might need to be prepared to handle possible crashes.
Messages sent from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
to a crashed
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
are lost
– but if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
tries to receive from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
can detect that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
has crashed, and take a crash handling branch.
Meanwhile, another process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}
may (or may not) have detected
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
’s crash,
and may be handling it – and in either case, any interaction between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}
should remain correct.

A key design feature of our framework is
to support
optional reliability assumptions
: a
programmer may declare that some peers will
never
crash for the duration
of the protocol.
Such optional assumptions allow for simplifying protocols and programs: if a
participant is declared reliable, its peers can interact with it
without implementing crash detection and handling.
Moreover, by
making such assumptions explicit and customisable,
our theory supports a

spectrum
of assumptions ranging from only having sessions
with reliable participants (as in classic MPST works
[
HYC16
,
POPL19LessIsMore
]
),
to having no reliable participants at all.

Another fundamental aspect of our theory is its adherence to a
top-down
methodology,
which stems from the original MPST theory
[
HYC08
]
.
This approach starts the design of multiparty session types
with a given
global
type
(top),
and processes rely on
local
types (bottom) obtained from the
global type.
The global and local types reflect the global and local communication
behaviours respectively.
Well-typed processes that conform to a global type are
guaranteed to be
correct by construction
,
enjoying full guarantees (safety, deadlock-freedom, and liveness) from the theory.

The use of global types in our design for handling failures in multiparty session types
presents three distinct advantages:

{enumerate*}
[label=
(0)
]

there is no user-level syntax extension of global types compared with
the original MPST global types, apart from a special label to signify crash
handling branches;

global types provide a simple, high-level means to both specify a protocol
abstractly and automatically derive local types; and,

under (optional) reliability assumptions, desirable behavioural properties such as communication safety,
deadlock-freedom, and liveness are guaranteed by construction.

In contrast to the
synchronous
semantics used by Barwell

et al.

[
CONCUR22MPSTCrash
]
, we model
asynchronous
semantics,
where messages can be buffered whilst in transit.
We focus on asynchronous systems
since most communication in the real distributed world is asynchronous.
Although Le Brun and Dardha
[
ESOP23MAGPi
]
develop a generic typing system incorporating asynchronous semantics,
their approach results in type-level properties
becoming undecidable
[
ESOP23MAGPi
, §4.4]
.
With global types,
we restore decidability at a minor cost to
expressivity.

Outline

This article represents an extended rendition of the work on
asynchronous multiparty session type theory with
crash-stop failures as initially introduced in
[
DBLP:conf/ecoop/BarwellHY023
]
.
Our enhanced version provides a more comprehensive and refined presentation, offering detailed definitions,
an in-depth exploration of related work, and additional examples.
Moreover, we incorporate a new section (Section
) that illustrates our approach
through an extensive case study of the Non-Blocking Atomic Commits abstraction,
described in
[
DBLP:books/daglib/0025983
]
.
In contrast to
[
DBLP:conf/ecoop/BarwellHY023
]
,
we have omitted the implementation of our theory, as our primary focus is on the theoretical aspects.

Note that, for clarity and simplicity, our framework does not support delegation (session passing), as our primary focus is on developing a top-down methodology for handling failures in multiparty session types. However, all the results presented in the paper are applicable to systems that do incorporate delegation.
For readers interested in the generalised MPST theory that includes both
crash-stop failures and delegation,
please refer to
[
CONCUR22MPSTCrash
]
.

Section
2
We begin with an overview of our methodology.

Section
3
Addressing the challenge of defining
the semantics of crashing and crash handling
within the context of the crash-stop model,
we introduce an asynchronous multiparty session
calculus with minimal syntax changes.

Section
4
We introduce an extended theory of asynchronous multiparty session
types with semantic modelling of crash-stop failures.
The challenges here involve capturing the non-trivial crash-stop semantics
of global and local types under
optional
reliability assumptions,
as well as demonstrating that the sound and
complete operational correspondence between the two semantics
(Theorems
9
and
10
) can ensure desirable
global type properties such as communication safety, deadlock-freedom, and
liveness in local types through projection,
even in the presence of crashes (
14
).

Section
5
We present a typing system for our asynchronous multiparty session calculus.
The challenges comprise proving behavioural properties for typed sessions: subject reduction, session fidelity, deadlock-freedom, and liveness, in corresponding theorems
(Theorems
,
,
, and

).

Section
We provide a case study on the Non-Blocking Atomic Commits abstraction,
which facilitates the reliable execution of transactions in distributed database systems.

We discuss related work in
Section
and conclude in

Section
.

2.
Overview

In this work,
we follow a standard top-down design approach enabling
correctness by construction
, but enrich asynchronous MPST with crash-stop semantics. As depicted in
Fig.

1
, we formalise (asynchronous) multiparty protocols with crash-stop failures
as global types with
crash handling branches
(
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
). These are projected onto local types, which may similarly contain crash handling branches (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
).
The projected local types are, in turn, used to type-check
processes (also with crash handling branches (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
)) that are written in a session calculus.
As an example, we consider a simple

distributed logging
scenario, which is inspired by
the logging-management protocol
[
ECOOP22AffineMPST
]
, but extended with a third
participant.

A Global Type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}

\textstyle\text{with }{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
projection
(
\upharpoonright
)
Local Type for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}

[formula]
Local Type for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}

\textstyle\text{with }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}

[formula]
Local Type for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}

[formula]
typing
(
\vdash
)
Process for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}

[formula]
Process for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}

\textstyle\text{with }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}

[formula]
Process for
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}

[formula]

Figure 1
.
Top-down view of MPST with crash.

The Simpler Logging protocol consists of a
logger
(
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
) that controls the
logging services, an
interface
(
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
) that provides communications between
logger and client, and a
client
(
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
) that requires logging services via interface.
Initially, the logger process
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
sends a heartbeat message
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{trigger}}
to the
interface process
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
. Then the client
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
sends a command to
the interface
to read the logs (
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{read}}
).
When a
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{read}}
request is
sent, it is forwarded to the logger, and the logger responds
with a
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{report}}
, which is then forwarded onto the client.
When all participants (i.e. logger, interface, and client) are assumed reliable, i.e. without failures or crashes,
this logging behaviour can be represented by the
global type

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{0}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{0}}\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{trigger}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{read}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{read}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{report}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{report}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathsf{end}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}

Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{0}}
is a specification of the Simpler Logging protocol between multiple roles from
a global perspective.

In the real distributed world, all participants in the Simpler Logging system
may fail. Ergo, we may need to model protocols with failures or
crashes and handling behaviour thereof, e.g. should the client fail after the logging has started, the interface
will inform the logger to stop and exit.
We follow
[
DBLP:books/daglib/0025983
, §2.2]
to model
a
crash-stop
semantics, where we assume that roles can crash
at any time
unless assumed

reliable
(never fail or crash).
For simplicity, we assume the interface
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
and the logger
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
to be reliable.
The above logging behaviour, incorporating crash-stop failures, can be represented by extending

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{0}}
with a branch handling crashes of
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{trigger}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{:}\left\{{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{read}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{read}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{report}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{report}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathsf{end}}}}}}}}}}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{crash}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{fatal}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathsf{end}}}}}}}\end{array}}\right\}}}}}

(1)

We model crash detection on receiving roles: when a role
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
is
waiting to receive a message from role
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
, the receiving role
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
is
able to detect whether
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
has
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
ed.
Since crashes are detected only by the receiving role, we do not
require a crash handling branch on the communication step between
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
and

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
– nor do we require them on any interaction between
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
(since we
are assuming that
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
are reliable).
Note that our global type syntax has minimal changes from the standard syntax,
with additional crashing and crash handling semantics. We give details
in Section
4
.

Following the MPST top-down methodology,
a global type is then
projected
onto
local types
,
which describe communications from the perspective of a single role.
In our unreliable Simpler Logging example,

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
is projected onto three local types (one for each role

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
):

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}={\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{read}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{report({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}})}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}}={\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{trigger}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{read}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{report({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}})}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{fatal}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}\end{array}\right\}}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}={\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{trigger}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{read}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{read}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{report({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}})}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{report({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{log}})}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{fatal}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}\end{array}\right\}}\end{array}

Here,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}
states that the interface

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
first receives a trigger message from the logger

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
; then
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
either expects a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{read}}
request from
the client
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
, or detects the crash of
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}
and handles it (in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
)
by sending the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{fatal}}
message to notify the logger
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}
.
Again, the syntax of local types does not depart from the standard, but we add
additional crash modelling and introduce a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{stop}}
type for crashed
endpoints, explained in Section
4
.
We show the
operational correspondence between global and local type
semantics in Theorems
9
and
10
,
and demonstrate that
a projectable global type always produces a safe, deadlock-free,
and live typing context in
14
.

The next step in this top-down methodology is to use
local types to type-check processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}
executed by role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}}

in our session calculus.
For example,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}
can be used to type check the interface

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}
that executes the process:

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}?\mathtt{trigger}.\sum\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}?\mathtt{read}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}!\mathtt{read}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}?\mathtt{report}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}).{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}!\mathtt{report}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\\
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}!\mathtt{fatal}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\end{array}\right\}}}

In our operational semantics (Section
3
),
we allow active processes executed by unreliable roles
to crash
arbitrarily
.
Therefore, the role executing the crashed process
also crashes, and is assigned the local type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{stop}}
.
To ensure that a communicating process is type-safe even in the presence of crashes,
we require that its typing context satisfies a
safety property
accounting for possible crashes (
subsection 4.6
),
which is preserved by
projection (
14
).
Despite minor changes in the surface syntax of types and processes,
additional semantics surrounding crashes adds subtleties even in standard
results. We prove subject reduction and session fidelity results, accounting for crashes and
sets of reliable roles, in Theorems
and
.

3.
Asynchronous Multiparty Session Calculus with Crash-Stop Semantics

In this section, we formalise the syntax (Section
3.1
) and operational semantics (Section
3.2
)
of our asynchronous multiparty session calculus with process failures and crash detection.
For clarity of presentation, delegation is not supported in our system.

3.1.
Syntax of Session Calculus with Crash-Stop Failures

Our asynchronous multiparty session calculus models processes that may crash arbitrarily.
Our formalisation is based on
[
POPL21AsyncMPSTSubtyping
]
– but
additionally follows the
fail-stop
model in
[
DBLP:books/daglib/0025983
, §2.7]
,
where processes may crash and never recover, and where process failures can be detected by failure detectors
[
DBLP:books/daglib/0025983
, §2.6.2]

[
JACM96FailureDetector
]
when attempting to receive messages.

The syntax of our calculus is presented in
Fig.

2
. Values and expressions are standard: a
value
, ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v^{\prime}},\ldots
,
can be a natural number
n
,
an integer
i
,
a boolean
true
or
false
,
a string
""
,
a unit
()
(often omitted for brevity),
or any other specifically tailored value;
an
expression
, ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e^{\prime}},\ldots
,
can be a variable, a value,
or a term built from expressions by applying operators, such as

\text{{succ}},\text{{neg}},\neg
, and
<
.
We fix a set of
roles

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
, ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}},\ldots
.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{{n}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{{i}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{\text{{true}}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{\text{{false}}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\text{\text{{"{}"}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\text{{({})}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\cdots\\[1.99168pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}\Coloneqq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\text{{succ}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}})\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\text{{neg}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}})\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}<{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e^{\prime}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\cdots\\[4.2679pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}ll@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}&\Coloneqq&&\text{\bf Processes}\\
&&\sum_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{i}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}_{i}&\text{external choice}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\quad\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle\text{(where }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}\text{)}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle\text{(where }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}\text{)}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle\text{(where }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}\text{)}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle\text{(where }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}\text{)}$}}&\text{output}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{if}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}}\,\mathsf{then}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\,\mathsf{else}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}&\text{conditional}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}&\text{process variable}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}&\text{recursion}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}&\text{inaction}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}$}}&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle\text{crashed}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle\text{crashed}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle\text{crashed}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle\text{crashed}$}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}ll@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}&\Coloneqq&&\text{\bf Sessions}\\
&&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}&\text{participant}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}&\text{parallel}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}&\Coloneqq&&\text{\bf Queues}\\
&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}&\text{empty }\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}$}}&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle\text{unavailable }$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle\text{unavailable }$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle\text{unavailable }$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle\text{unavailable }$}}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&\left({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v})\right)&\text{message}\\
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}&\text{concatenation}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 2
.
Syntax of values, expressions, sessions, processes, and queues.
Appreciable changes w.r.t. the standard session calculus
[
POPL21AsyncMPSTSubtyping
]
are
highlighted
.

A process, ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q},\ldots
, is a communication agent within a
session.
An
output
process

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}

sends a message to another role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
in the session,
where the message is labelled
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}
, and carries a payload expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}
,
then the process continues as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
.
An
external choice
(
input
) process

\sum_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{i}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}_{i}

receives a message from another role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
in the session,
among a finite set of indices
I
,
if the message is labelled
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathtt{m}}_{\mathnormal{i}}}
, then the payload would be received as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x}_{i}}
, and the process continues as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}
.
Note that our calculus uses
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
as a special message label
denoting that a participant (role) has crashed.
Such a label cannot be sent by any process, but a process can implement
crash detection and handling by receiving it.
Consequently, an output process
cannot
send
a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
message (side condition
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
),
whereas an input process
may include a
crash handling branch
of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}

meaning that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}
is executed when the sending role has crashed.
A
conditional
process

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{if}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}}\,\mathsf{then}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\,\mathsf{else}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}

continues as either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
depending on the evaluation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}

(which is detailed later in Section
3.2
).
We allow
recursion
at the process level using

\mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
, requiring process recursion variables to be guarded by an input or
output action.
Finally, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
for an
inactive
process, representing
a successful termination; and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}
for a
crashed
process, representing a
termination due to failure.

An
incoming queue
1
1

1

In
[
POPL21AsyncMPSTSubtyping
]
, the queues are outgoing
instead of incoming.
We use incoming queues to model our crashing semantics more easily.
, ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{i}},\ldots
, is a sequence of messages
tagged with their origin.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}
for an
empty
queue;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}
for an
unavailable
queue;
and
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}({v}))

for a message sent from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, labelled
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}
, and containing a payload
value
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{1}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{2}}
to denote the concatenation of two queues

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{2}}
.
When describing incoming queues, we consider two messages from different
origins as swappable:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{1}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{1}({v}_{1}))\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{2}({v}_{2}))\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{2}

is structurally equivalent to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{1}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{2}({v}_{2}))\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{1}({v}_{1}))\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{2}

whenever
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}}
.
Moreover, we consider concatenation
(\cdot)
as associative, and the empty
queue
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}
as the identity element for concatenation.

A session, ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathbb{M}}_{i}},\ldots
, consists of processes and their
respective incoming queue, indexed by their roles.
A single entry for a role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
is denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
is the process for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}
is
the incoming queue.
Entries are composed together in parallel as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
, where the
roles in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}
are disjoint.
We consider parallel composition as commutative and associative, with

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}
as a neutral
element of the operator.
We write

\prod_{i\in I}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{i}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}_{i}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{i}})

for the parallel composition of multiple entries in a set.

3.2.
Operational Semantics of Session Calculus with Crash-Stop Failures

The
evaluation
of an expression is illustrated in
Figure

3
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}\downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}
indicates that the expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}
evaluates to the value
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}
,
and an
evaluation context

\mathcal{E}
is an expression containing exactly one hole.
The successor operation
succ
is defined only for
natural numbers,
the negation
neg
is defined for integers, the logical negation
\neg
is defined only
for boolean values, and the less-than operator
<
is applied only to integers.

We give the operational semantics of our session calculus in
subsection 3.2
,
using a
structural precongruence

\Rrightarrow
defined in
Figure

5
.
Structural precongruence is a
preorder
that relates sessions based on inconsequential structural modifications.
A standard structural congruence
\equiv
can be defined as the symmetric closure of
\Rrightarrow
:
\Rrightarrow\cup\Rrightarrow^{-}
.

Our semantics parameterises on a (possibly empty) set of
reliable
roles
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}
,
i.e. roles assumed to
never crash
.
This approach enables us to model
optional reliability
, allowing for the representation of
a
spectrum
of reliability assumptions
that range from total process reliability (as seen in standard session types
work, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
)
to total unreliability (i.e.
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}=\emptyset
).

{defi}

[Session Reductions]

The session reduction relation
\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}

is inductively
defined by the rules in
Fig.

4
,
parameterised by a fixed set
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}
of reliable roles.
We write
\to

whenever
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}
is not significant.
We write
\mathrel{\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}^{*}}
(resp.
\mathrel{\to^{*}}
)
for the reflexive and transitive closure of
\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}
(resp.
\to
).

Our operational semantics retains the basic rules
in
[
POPL21AsyncMPSTSubtyping
]
, but also includes (
highlighted
)
rules for crash-stop
failures and crash handling, adapted from
[
CONCUR22MPSTCrash
]
.
Rules
[r-send]
and
[r-rcv]
model ordinary message delivery
and reception:
an output process located at
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
sending to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
would append a
message to the incoming queue of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
; and an input process located at

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
receiving from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
would consume the first message from the
incoming queue.
Rules
[r-cond-T]
and
[r-cond-F]
model the conditional
process; and rule
[r-struct]
permits reductions up to structural
precongruence.

With regard to crashes and related behaviour, rule
[r-
\lightning
]

models process crashes: an active (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
) process located at an
unreliable role (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\notin{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}
) may reduce to a crashed process

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}
, with its incoming queue becoming unavailable

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}
.
Rule
[r-send-
\lightning
]
models a
message delivery to a crashed role (and thus an unavailable queue),
and the message becomes lost and would not be added to the queue.
Rule
[r-rcv-
\odot
]
models crash detection, which activates as a
‘last resort’:
an input process at
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
receiving from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
would first attempt find a
message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
in the incoming queue, which engages the usual rule

[r-recv]
;
if none exists and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
has crashed
(
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}
), then the crash handling branch in the input
process at
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
can activate.
We draw attention to the interesting fact that
[r-recv]
may engage
even if
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
has crashed, in cases where a message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
in the
incoming queue may be consumed.

&\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle\to\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}_{k}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle\to\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}_{k}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle\to\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}_{k}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb {\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{{\scriptsize[r-cond-T]}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{if}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}}\,\mathsf{then}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}\,\mathsf{else}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\;\to\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}&\hskip-280.00043pt({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}\downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{\text{{true}}}})\\[2.84526pt]

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{{\scriptsize[r-struct]}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{1}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{\prime}\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{1}^{\prime}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{2}^{\prime}\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{2}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{2}\quad\implies\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{1}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}_{2}\end{array}

.
Reduction relation on sessions with crash-stop failures.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{1}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{i}({v}_{i}))\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{j},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{j}({v}_{j}))\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{2}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{1}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{j},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{j}({v}_{j}))\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{i}({v}_{i}))\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}_{2}\\[2.84526pt]
\text{(if $i\neq j$, $i,j\in\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{1,2\right\}}}$, ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{1}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}_{2}$)}\\[4.2679pt]

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\quad\mu X.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathord{\left\{{\nicefrac{{\mu X.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}}}{{X}}}\right\}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\;\text{ and }\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime}}\;\implies\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime}}\\[4.2679pt]

Figure 5
.
Structural precongruence rules for queues, processes, and sessions.

{exa}

We now illustrate our operational semantics of sessions with an example.
Consider the session:

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}\langle\text{``{abc}''}\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}

with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}=\sum\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\end{array}\right\}}}
,
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}=\sum\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\end{array}\right\}}}

with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}}\langle 42\rangle.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}
.

In this session, the process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
receives a message sent from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
;
the process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
sends a message from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
, and then receives a message
sent from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.

On a successful reduction (without crashes), we have:

\begin{array}[]{rll}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}&\to&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}(\text{``{abc}''}))\\
&\to&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{\prime}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\\
&\to&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}^{\prime}}(42))\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\\
&\to&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\end{array}

Let
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}=\emptyset
(i.e. each role is unreliable).
Suppose that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
crashes before sending, which leads to the reduction:

\begin{array}[]{rll}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}&\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\\
&\to&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}\end{array}

We can observe that when the output (sending) process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}
located at an unreliable role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
crashes (by

[r-
\lightning
]
),
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
also crashes (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}
), with an unavailable incoming message
queue (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}
). Subsequently, the input (receiving) process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
located at
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
can detect and
handle the crash by
[r-rcv-
\odot
]
via its handling branch.

4.
Asynchronous Multiparty Session Types with Crash-Stop Semantics

In this section, we present our asynchronous multiparty session types
with crash-stop semantics.
We give an overview of global and local types with crashes in Section
4.1
,
including syntax, projection, and subtyping.
Our key additions to the classic theory are
crash handling branches
in
both global and local types,
and a special local type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{stop}}
to denote crashed processes.
We give a Labelled Transition System (LTS) semantics to both global types
(Section
4.2
) and configurations (i.e. a collection of local types
and point-to-point communication queues, Section
4.3
).
We discuss alternative design options of modelling crash-stop
failures in Section
4.4
.
We relate the two semantics in Section
4.5
, and show that
a configuration obtained via projection is safe, deadlock-free, and live in Section
4.6
.

4.1.
Global and Local Types with Crash-Stop Failures

The top-down methodology begins with
global types
to provide an
overview of
the communication between a number of
roles

(
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{t}}}}},\ldots
),
belonging to a (fixed) set
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
.
On the other end, we use
local types
to describe how a
single

role communicates with other roles from a local perspective, and they are
obtained via
projection
from a global type.
We give the syntax of both global and local types in
Fig.

6
,
which are similar to syntax used in
[
POPL19LessIsMore
,
CONCUR22MPSTCrash
]
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}c@{\quad}l@{\quad}l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{nat}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{int}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{bool}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{str}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\operatorname{unit}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\ldots&\text{\small Basic types}\\[1.99168pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q^{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}$}}}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}&{\small\text{Transmission}}\\[1.99168pt]

I}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!30}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}}}}}}}}{\rightsquigarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}{j}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in \end{array}

Figure 6
.
Syntax of basic types, global types, and local types. Runtime types are

shaded
.

Basic Types

Basic types
(types for payloads) are taken from a set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathcal{B}}
,
and describe the types of values such as natural numbers, integers, booleans, strings, and units.

Global Types

Global types

are ranged over
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}},\ldots
,
and describe the behaviour for all roles from a bird’s eye view.
The syntactic constructs shown in
shade
are
runtime
constructs, which are not used for
describing a system at design-time, but for describing the state of a system
during execution.

We explain each global type syntactic construct:
a transmission

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}}}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}

denotes a message from role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
to role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
(with possible crash
annotations), with labels
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}
, payload types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}
,
and continuations
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}
, where
i
is taken from an index set
I
.
We require that the index set be non-empty (
I\neq\emptyset
), labels

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}
be pair-wise distinct and taken from a fixed set of labels
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathcal{M}}
,
and self receptions be excluded (i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
), as is standard in session type literature. Additionally, we require that the special
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
label (explained later)
not be the only label in a transmission, i.e.
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}\,\middle|\,{i\in I}\right\}}}\neq\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}\right\}}}
.
A transmission en route

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}}}}}}}}{\rightsquigarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}{j}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}

is a runtime construct representing a message
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{j}}
(index
j
) sent by

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, and yet to be received by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
.
Recursive types are represented via
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{G}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}
,
where contractive requirements apply
[
PierceTAPL
, §21.8]
.
The type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathsf{end}}
describes a terminated type (omitted where
unambiguous).

To model crashes and crash handling, we use crash annotations
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}

and crash handling branches:
a
crash annotation

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}
, a new addition in this work, marks
a
crashed
role (only used in the
runtime syntax
), and we omit
annotations for
live
(or
active
) roles, i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
is a live role,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}}}}}}
is a
crashed role, and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}}}}}}}
represents a possibly crashed role,
namely either
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}}}}}}
.
We use a special label
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
for handling crashes: this
continuation denotes the protocol to follow when the sender of a
message is detected to have crashed by the receiver.
The special label acts as a ‘pseudo’-message: when a sender role crashes, the
receiver can select the ‘pseudo’-message to enter crash handling.

{defi}

[Set of Live and Crashed Roles]

The set of
live
roles in a global
type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
,
and the set of
crashed
roles, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
,
are defined inductively as:

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}l@{\;}lr@{\;}l@{\;}l}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}&=&\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\right\}}}\cup\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}})}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}&=&\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}})}}\\[1.42262pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}&=&\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\right\}}}\cup\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}})}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}&=&\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\right\}}}\cup\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}})}}\\[1.42262pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}}}}}}}}{\rightsquigarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}{j}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}&=&\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\right\}}}\cup\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}})}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}}}}}}}}{\rightsquigarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}{j}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}&=&\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}})}}\\[1.42262pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathsf{end}}})}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}})}&=&\emptyset&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathsf{end}}})}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}})}&=&\emptyset\\[1.42262pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}})}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\mathord{\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}}}\right\}}})}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}})}&=&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\mathord{\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}}}\right\}}})}\end{array}

Remark 1
.

Even if the sender
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
occurs as crashed during a communication in
transit, it is not considered
as crashed unless it appears
crashed in a continuation:

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}}}}}}}{\rightsquigarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}{j}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\lightning}}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}})}}.

Local Types

Local types
(or
session types
) are ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}S},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}U},\ldots
, and describe the behaviour of a single role.
An internal choice (selection)

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{i}}\right\}_{i\in I}}

(resp. an external choice (branching)

{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{i}}\right\}_{i\in I}}
)
indicates that the
current
role is to
send
to (resp.
receive
from) the role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Similarly to global types, we require pairwise-distinct,
non-empty labels in local types.
Moreover, we require that the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
label not appear in
internal

choices, reflecting that a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
‘pseudo’-message can never be
sent; and that singleton
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
labels are not permitted in external choices.
The type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}
indicates a
successful
termination (omitted where
unambiguous), and recursive types follow a similar fashion to global types.
We use a new
runtime
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{stop}}
to denote crashes.

Typographical Conventions

Whenever a payload type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B}
is insignificant, we choose to omit it from
the global or local type.
This is frequently the case when we discuss the branches with
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
(in
a global type) or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}
(in a local types) labels,
where the payload type is irrelevant.

Projection

Projection
gives the local type of a participating role in a global
type,
defined as a
partial
function that takes a global type

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
and a
role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
to project on, and returns a local type, given by
subsection 4.1
.

{defi}

[Global Type Projection]The
projection of a global type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
onto a role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, with respect to a set of
reliable
roles
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}
,
written
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\upharpoonright_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}}
,  is:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\left({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\dagger}}}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}\right)}\upharpoonright_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}}=\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\hskip {\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}B_{i}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{i}}}\upharpoonright_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}})\right\}_{i\in I}}\hfil\hskip 8.53581pt&\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{}}\text{\footnotesize if \,}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}},\text{\footnotesize\,and \,}\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\notin{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}\text{\footnotesize\, implies}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\notin{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}\text{\footnotesize\, implies}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\notin{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}\text{\footnotesize\, implies}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow!40}$\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\notin{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}\text{\footnotesize\, implies}$}}\\
