${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-,{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}\right)}$ (resp. ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-,{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}\right)}$, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-,{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}\right)}$),
which can be verified in the standard way. Then, together with the association
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}\right\}}}}}{\langle{\emptyset};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{s}}}\rangle}}$,
we can use {\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{{\scriptsize[{t-sess}]}}} to assert that the session
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}$
is governed by the global type
$\langle{\emptyset};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{s}}}\rangle$.
If we follow a crash reduction, e.g.{} by the rule {\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{{\scriptsize[r-$\lightning$]}}},
the session evolves as ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}\;\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}\;{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{L}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{I}}}}}}$, where, by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\text{{\scriptsize[t-${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lightning}$]}}}, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}$ is typed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{stop}}$, and
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{C}}}}}}$ is typed by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oslash}$.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Properties of Typed Sessions}
We present the main properties of typed sessions: \emph{subject reduction} (\autoref{lem:sr}),
\emph{session fidelity} (\autoref{lem:sf}), \emph{deadlock-freedom} (\autoref{lem:session_deadlock_free}), and
\emph{liveness} (\autoref{lem:session_live}).
\par\par\par\emph{Subject reduction} states that well-typedness of sessions is preserved by reduction, i.e.{} a session that is governed by a global type continues to be
governed by a global type.
\par\begin{thm}If
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$\;
and
\;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}$,\;
then either
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}$,\;
or there exists \;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle$\; such that
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle$\;
and
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}$.
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}By induction on the derivation of ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}$.
See Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:typesystem} for details.
\end{proof}
\par\emph{Session fidelity} states the opposite implication with regard to subject reduction:
sessions respect the progress of the governing global type.
\par\par\begin{thm}If
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$\; and
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}$,\;
then there exists ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}$ and \;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle$\; such that
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle$,
\;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\mathrel{\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}^{*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}$\; and
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}\rangle\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}$.
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}By induction on the derivation of $\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}$.
See Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:typesystem} for details.
\end{proof}
\par Session \emph{deadlock-freedom} means that
the `successful' termination of a session may include crashed processes and their respective unavailable
incoming queues -- but reliable roles (which cannot crash) can only successfully terminate by reaching
inactive processes with empty incoming queues.
We formalise the definition of deadlock-free sessions in~\autoref{def:session_df} and show that a well-typed session is deadlock-free
in~\autoref{lem:session_deadlock_free}.
\par\defi[Deadlock-Free Sessions]
A session ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$is\emph{deadlock-free}iff\;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\mathrel{\to_{${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}$}^{*}} {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}'}\mathrel{\not{\!\!\to_{${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}$}}}$\;implieseither\;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}'}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}$forsome${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$}$,\;or\;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}'}\Rrightarrow\prod_{i\in I} ({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$ _{i}} \triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\lightning$} \mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}$ _{i}} \triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\oslash})$with$I \neq\emptyset$.\par\par\begin{thm}If
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$,\;
then ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$ is deadlock-free.
\end{thm}\begin{proof}The result follows by~\autoref{lem:ext-proj}~(deadlock-freedom by projection),
\autoref{lem:sr}~(subject reduction), and~\autoref{lem:sf}~(session fidelity).
See Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:typesystem} for details.
\end{proof}\par Finally,weshowthatwell-typedsessionsguaranteethepropertyof\emph{liveness}:asessionis\emph{live}whenallitsinputprocesseswillbeperformedeventually,andallitsqueuedmessageswillbeconsumedeventually.Weformalisethedefinitionoflivesessionsin~\autoref{def:session_live}andconcludebyshowingthatawell-typedsessionislivein~\autoref{lem:session_live}.\par\defi[LiveSessions]Asession${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$ is \emph{live} iff
\;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}\mathrel{\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}^{*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\Rrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime}}$\;
implies:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@if \;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}=\left({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v})\right)\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}$, \;then $\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}^{*}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@if \;${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}=\sum_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{m}}_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{i}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}_{i}$, \;then $\exists k\in I,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\to_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}}^{*}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}_{k}\mathord{\left\{{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{k}}}}\right\}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}}$.
\end{enumerate}
\par\begin{thm}If
\;$\langle{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{C}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$,\;
then ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{M}}$ is live.
\end{thm}
\begin{proof}The result follows by~\autoref{lem:ext-proj}~(liveness by projection), \autoref{lem:sr}~(subject reduction), and~\autoref{lem:sf}~(session fidelity).
See Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:typesystem} for details.
\end{proof}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{retry}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{accept}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{abort}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{enact}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}\end{cases}}\end{cases}}\\[31.29802pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{reject}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{abort}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{retry}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{reject}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{abort}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{end}}}\end{cases}}\end{cases}}}}}\end{tabular}$$\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{11}}{Projected local types for the NBAC abstraction ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 11}}{\small Projected local types for the NBAC abstraction ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$.}}}
\end{figure}
\par\par\par\par We demonstrate our approach via the Non-Blocking Atomic Commits (NBAC)
abstraction~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:books/daglib/0025983}{}{}]}, which enables consistent
application of transactions in distributed database systems. The abstraction is
straightforward: a given transaction is applied iff all data managers -- each
representing part of the database -- accept the transaction.
We present a variant of this abstraction via the global type ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$ in
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:case:gtype}, which involves a co-ordinator ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$, two data managers
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}$, and one unreliable leading data manager ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$.
The
protocol exhibits failover behaviour, where ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ is the failover role for
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$. For clarity, we opt to eliminate the right-hand braces from global types.
\par\par The recursive protocol in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:case:gtype} begins with the co-ordinator ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$
broadcasting a transaction proposal, denoted ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{req}}$, to all three data
managers. For brevity, we omit payload types and write
${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{req}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}}}$ for
${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{req}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{req}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}{\to}{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{req}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}}}}}}}}}$.
Data managers ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}$ send their votes to the leading data manager ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$.
In cases where ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$ does not crash, it sends ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{reject}}$ to ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ when
it receives a ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{reject}}$ion from its peers, or if ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$ itself
${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{reject}}$s the transaction; ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ subsequently broadcasts an ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{abort}}$
message (in ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{2}}$) prior to the protocol recursing. In cases where ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$,
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$, and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}$ all vote to ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{accept}}$ the transaction, ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$
broadcasts a success message (in ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{1}}$).
In cases where ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ detects that ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$ has crashed, it promotes
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ to leader. Once promoted, ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ receives a vote from ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}$,
and informs ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ of the data managers' decision. Should the transaction be
rejected (resp.{} accepted), ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ broadcasts an abort (resp.{} a success)
message to all live data managers, before the protocol terminates.
The global types ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{4}}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{5}}$ define this crash handling behaviour.
\par\par\par All projected local types for the NBAC protocol ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$ are listed in
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:case:ltype}.
The type obtained by projecting onto ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$, ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}$, is the sole local
type that contains a crash handling branch since ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$ is the only unreliable
role, and both ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}$ do not receive messages from ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$.
Its crash handling branch is defined by ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{S}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}$. Similarly to the global
type above, broadcast operations are denoted as
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}_{n}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}}$, and stand for
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}_{1}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\dots\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}_{n}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}}.$
Additionally, we remove the right-hand braces from local types as well.
Projections for roles ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}$, ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$, and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}$ are as standard,
where labels ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{promote}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{retry}}$ trigger the crash handling
behaviour in ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}$ respectively.
\par\begin{figure}$$\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{req}}.\sum\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{accept}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{enact}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\\
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reject}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{abort}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\\
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}\end{array}\right\}}}}}\\[17.07164pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}&=&{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{promote}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{retry}}.\sum\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{accept}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{enact}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\\
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reject}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{abort}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\end{array}\right\}}}\\[17.07164pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}&=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{req}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{accept}}.\sum\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{enact}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X},\;{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{abort}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X},\;{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{promote}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}\end{array}\right\}}}}}\\[5.69054pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}&=&\sum\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{accept}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{accept}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{enact}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\\
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reject}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{reject}}.{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{abort}}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{0}}\end{array}\right\}}}\end{array}$$\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{12}}{Process definitions for roles ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ in ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 12}}{\small Process definitions for roles ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ in ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$.}}}
\end{figure}
\par Let ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}$ be the $\{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}\}$-safe configuration,
where
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\mathbin{\!\triangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}\mathbin{\!\triangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}\mathbin{\!\triangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}\mathbin{\!\triangleright\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{T}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}}}$,
and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\Delta}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\epsilon}}}$.
The processes ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}$ in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:case:prcss}, with
message queues ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}$
act as roles ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}$ and ${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}$ in ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$. The former,
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}$, has type ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}\right)}$; the latter,
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}$ will always accept a proposed transaction, and has type
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}\right)}$. Processes ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}$ can
be defined similarly such that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}}$ has type
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}\right)}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}$ has type
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}\right)}$.
Since the local types in ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma}$ are derived via projection from ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}$, we
have the association
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}},{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}\right\}}}}}{\langle{\emptyset};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle}}$.
This allows us to assert that the session
${\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{c}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{1}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}$, where ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{l}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}_{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{m_{2}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\epsilon}$,
is governed by the global type $\langle{\emptyset};{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\rangle$.
Consequently, this session is both deadlock-free (by~\autoref{lem:session_deadlock_free})
and live (by~\autoref{lem:session_live}).
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par
\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Related Work}
\par We summarise related work on session types with failure handling.
While most of the literature discussed includes delegation, our framework does not.
However, this distinction does not impact the core comparison, which focuses on failure handling within session types.
The discussion starts with
the closest related
work~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{OOPSLA21FaultTolerantMPST,FORTE22FaultTolerant,CONCUR22MPSTCrash,ESOP23MAGPi}{}{}]}
in which multiparty session types are extended to model crashes or failures.
\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Multiparty Session Types with Failures}
Peters \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{FORTE22FaultTolerant}{}{}]} propose an MPST framework to
model fine-grained unreliability.
In their work, each transmission in a global type is parameterised by a
reliability annotation,
which can be unreliable (sender/receiver can crash, and messages can be
lost),
weakly reliable (sender/receiver can crash, messages are not lost), or
reliable (no crashes or message losses).
The design choice taken in our work roughly falls under weakly
reliable in their work, where a ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}$ handling branch (in their work, a
default branch) needs to be present to handle failures.
In our work, the reliability assumptions operate on a coarse level, but
nonetheless are \emph{consistent} within a given global type -- if a role
${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}$ is assumed reliable, ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{\mathscr{R}}}}}$, then it does not crash for
the duration of the protocol, and vice versa.
Therefore, in a transmission ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}$, our model allows \emph{one}
of the two roles to be unreliable, whereas their work does not permit the
`mixing' of reliability of sending and receiving roles.
\par Viering \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{OOPSLA21FaultTolerantMPST}{}{}]} utilise MPST as a guidance for
fault-tolerant distributed systems with recovery mechanisms.
Their framework includes various features, such as sub-sessions,
event-driven programming, dynamic role assignments, and most importantly
failure handling.
Our work handles unreliability in distributed programming, with the
following differences.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Failure detection \emph{assumptions} and \emph{models} are different:
in our work, we assume a perfect failure detector, where all detected
crashes are genuine.
Their work uses a less strict assumption to allow \emph{false suspicions}.
This difference subsequently gives rise to how failures are handled in both
approaches:
in our work, we use a special ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{crash}}}$ handling branch in global types to specify how the global protocol should progress after a
crash has been detected.
In contrast, they use a \emph{try-catch} construct in global types.
In such a try-catch construct, crash detection within a sub-session
${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{1}}$ is specified
${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{1}}~\mathsf{with}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}@{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}~.~{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{2}}$, where ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{2}}$ is a
global type for failure handling (where ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}$ cannot occur), and
${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}$ is a \emph{monitor} that monitors ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}$ for possible failures.
Moreover, the well-formedness condition (2) in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{OOPSLA21FaultTolerantMPST}{}{}, \lx@sectionsign 4.1]} requires the first message in ${\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{G}_{2}}$ to
be a message broadcast of failure notification from the monitor ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}$
to all roles participating in the sub-session (except the crashed role
${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}$).
\par On this matter, we consider our framework more \emph{flexible} when
detecting and handling crashes: every communication construct can have a
crash handling branch (when the sender is not assumed reliable), and the
failure broadcast is not necessary (failure detection only occurs when
receiving from a crashed role).
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@The \emph{merge} operators, used when projecting global types to obtain
local types, are different: we use a more expressive \emph{full} merge
operator~(\autoref{def:local-type-merge}), whereas they use a \emph{plain}
merge operator, i.e.{} requiring all continuations to project to the
\emph{same} local type.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@\emph{Reliability assumptions} are different: in our work, we
support a range of assumptions from every role being unreliable, to totally
reliable (as in the literature).
In their work, they require \emph{at least one} reliable role, because
they use a \emph{monitoring tree} for detecting crashes.
Our work allows a role to detect the crash of its communication partner
during reception, thus requiring neither such trees nor reliable roles.
\end{enumerate}
\par Barwell \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CONCUR22MPSTCrash}{}{}]} develop a theory of multiparty session types with crash-stop
failures. Their theory models crash-stop failures in the semantics of processes
and session
types, where the type system uses a model checker to validate type safety.
Our theory follows a similar model of crash-stop failures, but differs in the
following ways.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@We model an asynchronous (message-passing) semantics, whereas they
model a synchronous (rendez-vous) semantics.
We focus on asynchronous systems, where a message can be buffered while in
transit, since most of the interactions in the real distributed world are asynchronous.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We follow a top-down methodology, beginning with protocol specification
using global types, whereas they follow~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{POPL19LessIsMore}{}{}]} to analyse
only local types.
Our method dispenses with the need to use a model checker.
More specifically, it is not feasible to model check asynchronous systems
with buffers, since the model may be infinite~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{POPL19LessIsMore}{}{}, Appendix \lx@sectionsign G]}.
\end{enumerate}
\par Le Brun and Dardha~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ESOP23MAGPi}{}{}]}
follow a similar framework
to~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CONCUR22MPSTCrash}{}{}]}.
They model an asynchronous semantics, and support more patterns of failure,
including message losses, delays, reordering, as well as link failures and
network partitioning.
However, their typing system suffers from its genericity, when type-level
properties become undecidable~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ESOP23MAGPi}{}{}, \lx@sectionsign 4.4]}.
Our work uses global types for guidance, and recovers decidability of
properties at a small expense of expressivity.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Other Session Types with Failures}
Most other session type works on modelling failures can be briefly categorised into
two: using \emph{affine types} or \emph{exceptions}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ECOOP22AffineMPST, LMCS18Affine,
DBLP:journals/pacmpl/FowlerLMD19}{}{}]}, and using \emph{coordinators} or \emph{supervision}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:conf/forte/AdameitPN17,ESOP18CrashHandling}{}{}]}.
The former adapts session types to an \emph{affine}
representation, in which endpoints may cease prematurely;
the latter, instead, are usually reliant on one or more \emph{reliable} processes that \emph{coordinate}
in the event of failure.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subparagraph}{toc}{\emph{Affine Failure Handling}}
Mostrous and Vasconcelos~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine}{}{}]}
first proposed the affine approach
to failure handling.
They present a $\pi$-calculus with affine binary sessions (i.e.{} limited to two participants)
and concomitant reduction rules that represent a
minimal extension to standard (binary) session types.
Their extension is primarily comprised of a \emph{cancel operator}, which is
semantically similar to our crash construct: it represents a process
that has terminated early.
Besides these similarities, our work differs from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine}{}{}]} in several ways.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@We address multiparty protocols and sessions rather than binary session types.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@In \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine}{}{}]}, a cancel operator can have an arbitrary session type;
consequently, crashes are not visible at the type level.
Instead, we type crashed session endpoints with the special type ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{stop}}$,
which lets us model crashes in the type semantics, and
helps us in ensuring that a process implements its failure handling as expected in its (global or local) type.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@The reduction rules of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine}{}{}]} do not permit a process to
terminate early arbitrarily: cancellations must be raised explicitly by the
programmer (or automatically by attempting to receive messages from crashed
endpoints).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Finally, cancellations in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine}{}{}]} may be caught and handled via a \emph{do-catch}
construct. This construct catches only the first cancellation and cannot be nested, thus providing little help in handling failure across
multiple roles. Our global and local types seamlessly support protocols where the failure of a role is detected (and handled) while handling the failure of another role.
\end{enumerate}
\par Fowler \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/FowlerLMD19}{}{}]}
present a concurrent
$\lambda$-calculus, \emph{EGV}, with asynchronous session-typed communication
and exception handling. Their approach is based on~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine}{}{}]}, and
therefore shares many of the same differences to our approach:
the use of the cancel operator and binary
session types, and the lack of a reduction rule enabling a process to crash
arbitrarily;
the cancel operator used in EGV takes an arbitrary session type
-- whereas we reflect the crashed status with the dedicated ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathsf{stop}}$ type.
Similar to our work, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:journals/pacmpl/FowlerLMD19}{}{}]}
has asynchronous communication channels:
messages are queued when sent, and delivered at a later stage.
\par Lagaillardie \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ECOOP22AffineMPST}{}{}]}
propose a framework of
\emph{affine} multiparty session types.
They utilise the affine type
system to enforce
that failures are handled.
In their system, a multiparty session can terminate prematurely.
While their theory can be used to model crash-stop failures, such failures
are not built into the semantics, so manual encoding of failures is necessary.
Moreover, there is no way to recover from a cancellation (i.e.{} failure) besides
propagating the cancellation.
In our work, we provide the ability to follow a \emph{different} protocol
when a crash is detected, which gives rise to more flexibility and
expressivity.
\par There are some other works adopting \emph{exception-based} approaches, e.g.{} Capecchi \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:journals/mscs/CapecchiGY16}{}{}]} and
Carbone \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:conf/concur/CarboneHY08}{}{}]}
model failure
at the \emph{application} level (similar to~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine,DBLP:journals/pacmpl/FowlerLMD19}{}{}]})
by equipping processes with constructs to actively
produce or catch failures. Conversely, our approach models
arbitrary failures (such as hardware failures).
\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subparagraph}{toc}{\emph{Coordinator Model Failure Handling}}
Coordinator model approaches find their roots in work by
Demangeon \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:journals/fmsd/DemangeonHHNY15}{}{}]}.
In their model, global types are extended by \emph{interrupt blocks}
wherein one role may interrupt the protocol.
Interruptions are broadcast to all active roles within the block, and, once
interrupted, the roles follow a continuation specified separately.
Although their original intention was primarily to interrupt streaming behaviour,
interrupt blocks (or similar constructs) have been used
to model crashes and failure handling in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:conf/forte/AdameitPN17,ESOP18CrashHandling}{}{}]}.
\par\par Adameit \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:conf/forte/AdameitPN17}{}{}]}
extend the standard MPST syntax with \emph{optional blocks}, representing regions of a protocol that are susceptible to communication failures.
In their approach, if a process ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}$ expects a value from an optional block which fails, then a
\emph{default value} is provided to ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}$, so ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}$ can continue running.
This ensures termination and deadlock-freedom.
Although this approach does not feature an explicit reliable coordinator
process, we describe it here due to the inherent coordination required for multiple processes to start and end an optional block. Our approach differs in three key ways.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@We model crash-stop process failures instead of impermanent link failures.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We extend the semantics of communications in lieu of introducing a
new syntactic construct to enclose the potentially crashing regions of a protocol --
our global type projections and typing context safety ensure that crash detection is performed at every pertinent communication point.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We allow crashes to significantly affect the evolution of protocols:
our global and local types can have crash detection branches specifying
significantly different behaviours w.r.t.{} non-crashing executions.
Conversely, the approach in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:conf/forte/AdameitPN17}{}{}]} does not
discriminate between the presence and absence of failures: both have the
same protocol in the optional block's continuation. \end{enumerate}
\par Viering \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ESOP18CrashHandling}{}{}]}
similarly extend the standard
global type syntax with a \emph{try-handle} construct, which is facilitated by the
presence of a reliable coordinator process, and via a construct to specify
reliable processes.
When the coordinator detects a failure, it broadcasts notifications to all remaining live processes;
then, the protocol proceeds according to the failure handling continuation
specified as part of the try-handle construct.
Our approach and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ESOP18CrashHandling}{}{}]} share several modelling choices: crash-stop
semantics, perfect links, and the possibility of specifying reliable processes.
However, unlike \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ESOP18CrashHandling}{}{}]}, our approach does \emph{not} depend on a reliable coordinator that broadcasts failure notifications: all roles in a protocol can be unreliable, all processes may crash.
\par Besides the differences discussed above, we decided not to adopt coordinator processes nor failure broadcasts in order to avoid their inherent drawbacks.
The use of a coordinator requires additional run-time resources and increases the overall complexity of a distributed system. Furthermore, the broadcasting of failure notifications introduces an effective synchronisation point for all roles, with additional overheads. Such synchronisation points may also make it harder to extend the theory to support scenarios
with unreliable communication.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subparagraph}{toc}{\emph{Alternative Session Types with Failure Handling}}
Caires and P{\'{e}}rez~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:conf/esop/CairesP17}{}{}]} present a linearly-typed
calculus that
supports non-determinism and process failures.
They present a core calculus with binary session types (based on linear logic),
extended with constructs permitting non-determinism and control effects (e.g.{} exceptions).
Failure is simulated via a non-deterministic choice operator.
The main difference is that we model arbitrary failures, and support
multiparty sessions.
\par Neykova and Yoshida~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{NY2017Recovery}{}{}]} build upon the \emph{Let it Crash}
and supervisor-based design of
the {Erlang} programming language.
Using an MPST specification, they build a dependency graph
between the processes interacting in a system; in case of failure,
this information is then used by supervisor processes
to determine which subset of processes should be restarted, and which messages
should be re-sent.
As in the coordinator model approaches above, {Erlang} supervisors result in
additional overhead compared with our approach.
Furthermore, as in the affine session types
approach~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LMCS18Affine,DBLP:journals/pacmpl/FowlerLMD19}{}{}]}, failure states
are not reflected in the type system. Instead, there is an underlying
assumption that the supervisors will restart (a subset of) the system
until it terminates successfully.
\par Chen \emph{et al.}{}~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DBLP:conf/forte/ChenVBZE16}{}{}]} handle partial failures in
MPST by transforming programs to include synchronisation
points, at which failures can be detected and handled.
Our approach is less rigid due to the handling of
failures: synchronisation points are not needed (unless required by the
protocol), and the original protocols do not need to meet certain criteria to
permit their transformation.
\par\par
\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion and Future Work}
To overcome the challenge of accounting for failure handling in
distributed systems using session types,
we present an asynchronous multiparty session type
framework with the ability to model and handle
crash-stop failures, e.g.{} caused by hardware faults.
In our session calculus, processes may crash
arbitrarily, and other processes can detect their crash and handle the event.
Additionally, our system supports the specification of \emph{optional
reliability assumptions}:
by influencing type-checking, they allow our approach
to cover the spectrum between idealised scenarios where no process ever fails
(as typically assumed in standard session type works),
and more realistic scenarios
where any process can fail at any time.
With only minimal changes to
the syntax of standard asynchronous MPST in our theory,
desirable global type properties such as deadlock-freedom, protocol conformance,
and liveness are preserved by construction in typed processes,
even in the presence of crashes.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par This work is a new step towards modelling and handling real-world failures
by utilising session types,
effectively bridging the gap between session type theory and
applications.
As future work, we plan to study different crash models (e.g.{} crash-recover) and
failures of other components (e.g.{} link failures).
These further steps would lead us to our longer term goal, i.e.{} to eventually
model and type-check implementations of well-known consensus algorithms used
in large-scale distributed systems.
